FINDINGS

1. (EXECUTED — this is the answer to Q1, and it is not the answer the
DIRECTIVE expected) The training set is 13 distinct prompts, not 900 pairs.
That, not the instrument's resolution, is what decides whether this experiment
can detect anything.

Measured directly:

  data/dpo_pairs.jsonl           pairs=1234   distinct tids=13   distinct prompts=13   max/tid=240
  data/dpo_pairs_capped.jsonl    pairs=918    distinct tids=13   distinct prompts=13   max/tid=120
  data/repair_pairs.jsonl        pairs=38     distinct tids=2    distinct prompts=2

Per-task breakdown of the 918: lps 120, simplify_path 120, decode_ways 120,
atoi 120, next_permutation 120, spiral 116, two_sum 64, balanced 43,
multiply_strings 40, three_sum 29, merge_intervals 14, word_break 7, roman 5.
Thirteen — the same count as the Task( constructors in forge.py (SEED_TASKS).
train_dpo.py:30 reads dpo_pairs.jsonl + repair_pairs.jsonl; that union is still
13 prompts.

Now the comparison the DIRECTIVE asked for. Every published verifier-grounded
preference-learning result I could verify at this method's shape has 3–4
orders of magnitude more prompt diversity:

  Work                        Data                                              Model / method                                         pass@1 delta
  DSTC, arXiv:2411.13611      50k instructions (oss-instruct-sd2-50k), J=8       StarCoder2-15B-instr / DeepSeek-Coder-33B-instr, DPO   SC2-15B: HumanEval+ 62.2→66.5 (+4.3), MBPP+ 60.9→61.9
                              samples each, self-generated tests and code        (β=0.2) via OpenRLHF, one epoch, full FT, 8×A100       (+1.0), BCB-hard 14.9→16.2 (+1.3). DSC-33B: HE+ 70.1→72.0
                                                                                                                                          (+1.9), MBPP+ 69.0→70.4 (+1.4), BCB-hard 19.6→22.3 (+2.7)
  CodeDPO, arXiv:2410.05605   114k pairs (93k correctness + 21k efficiency)      1.3B–7B, full FT                                       DSC-1.3B base 31.53 → 42.07 HumanEval; DSC-6.7B SFT
                                                                                                                                          73.17 → 83.54
  PLUM, arXiv:2406.06887      on-policy, test-case generated                     ~6.7–7B code LMs                                        abstract, verbatim: "increases pass rates by up to 4.8%
                                                                                                                                          on average on standard benchmarks and 11.8% on
                                                                                                                                          LiveCodeBench". MagiCoder-S-CL-7B HE 70.7→73.8, MBPP
                                                                                                                                          68.4→71.4
  ACECODER, arXiv:2502.01718, RL arm (not best-of-32)                            Qwen2.5-7B-Instr                                        HE 81.7→83.5 (+1.8), MBPP 79.4→83.1 (+3.7); Qwen2.5-
                                                                                                                                          Coder-7B-Instruct went HE 91.5→89.0 (−2.5)

The single most decisive number I found is CodeDPO's Table 7 data-scaling
ablation (DeepSeekCoder-1.3B-base, HumanEval pass@1): 25% → 32.92, 50% → 36.59,
75% → 41.46, 100% → 42.07. Its base model without DPO is 31.53. So the first
25% of the data — roughly 28,500 pairs — bought +1.4 points. The curve is
convex at the bottom, not concave: the paper's own "diminishing returns"
framing describes the top of the curve, but read from zero it says the
small-data end is where the method does least. srlm-forge's 918 pairs is
~1/32 of that first quartile, and over 13 prompts rather than tens of
thousands.

Compounding factors, all pointing the same way:
- LoRA r=16 vs the full fine-tuning used in every result above. Biderman et
  al., arXiv:2405.09673, abstract verbatim: "In the standard low-rank
  settings, LoRA substantially underperforms full finetuning" — measured on
  programming and math specifically, and full FT learns perturbations of "a
  rank that is 10-100X greater than typical LoRA configurations."
- DPO specifically is the unstable variant. PLUM reports PLUM-DPO sometimes
  underperforming PLUM-KTO and attributes it to DPO reducing the reward of
  the chosen class. ACECODER's RL arm produced a −2.5 point regression on one
  of three models. Negative outcomes are on the record for this family.
- The eval is out-of-distribution transfer. The 80 admitted eval tasks are
  AceCode oss problems; the 13 training prompts are hand-authored SEED_TASKS.
  This is not "did the model get better at the thing it trained on," it is
  "did training on 13 problems generalise to 80 unseen ones." §52 item 1
  already worries about skill overlap contaminating the measurement; the
  13-prompt count means the more likely failure is the opposite — no
  transfer at all.

Direct answer to Q1, stated plainly: the literature does not report pass@1
deltas for 8B / ~900 pairs / one epoch / LoRA r=16 / verifier-grounded DPO.
Nothing I could verify comes within two orders of magnitude of this data
scale. The closest analogue is DSTC (same signal — self-generated tests,
execution-verified pairs; same schedule — one epoch; same loss family), and it
reports +1.0 to +4.3 points at 50k instructions with full fine-tuning on
15B/33B models. Scaling that down by ~50× in prompt count and swapping full FT
for LoRA r=16, my estimate for a held-out instrument is +0.5 points central,
plausible range 0 to +2, with roughly 25–35% probability the true sign is
negative or indistinguishable from zero. Confidence in the point estimate:
low. Confidence in the ordering claim — that this is far below the 5–10
points the DIRECTIVE floated as the optimistic branch — is high, because it
rests on a measured scaling curve (CodeDPO Table 7) rather than on my
extrapolation.

One argument in the other direction, and I flag it as my reasoning, not
literature: every published delta above is measured on a partly-saturated
benchmark. The old srlm-forge instrument had 6 of 10 tasks pinned at 1.000, so
it registered ~40% of any real per-task movement. The new instrument holds
every task in [0.2, 0.8] where binomial variance and therefore sensitivity to
a capability shift are maximal. A capability change that shows as +1.5 on
HumanEval could plausibly show as +2–3 here. That is a factor I believe in
directionally and cannot size, and it is the reason I am not telling you the
experiment is unrunnable — only that it is being sized from the wrong end.

2. (EXECUTED) Your power arithmetic is correct and I could not break it —
including the subtle part. But it proves something different from what you
are using it for.

I re-derived §54 from the pilot file independently and it reproduces exactly:

  mean p(1-p) over the 30 admitted   = 0.2230
  per-task sd at 5 samples, computed as mean_i sqrt(p_i(1-p_i)/5) = 0.2108   (you: 0.2108)
  ratio to old 0.0928 = 2.272   count scale = 5.16   (you: 2.27 / 5.16)

The place this could have gone wrong is the definition of k, and I checked it
before believing you. eval.py:37 sets N_SAMPLES = 5, so your measured per-task
sd 0.0928 is already a 5-sample sd, not a single-draw sd. If k in your table
meant samples-per-task, the 30-task anchor would be double-counting a factor
of 5 and the real requirement would be ~775 tasks. It doesn't: k means eval
runs of 5 samples each, which your own §56 confirms arithmetically ("77 tasks
at k=10 … both ~3,850 generations" — 77 × 10 × 5 = 3,850). Under that reading
the closed form is

  G_per_arm = 2·(z_{.025}+z_{.20})²·mean(p(1-p)) / δ²   with T·k·N_SAMPLES = G
  δ=3pp →  3,889 generations/arm.   155 × 5 × 5 = 3,875.  ✓

Your 155 is right. §52's headline counts also reproduce exactly from the
pilot file (121 screened / 30 admitted 24.8% / 39 floor 32.2% / 36 ceiling
29.8% / 16 outside 13.2%, summing to 121 with zero unclassified).

But notice what the formula actually says: power depends on total generations
per arm, not on task count. Task count enters only through T·k·N. So the
"cheaper shape" you found in §54 is real and you stopped one step short of
its conclusion — 40 tasks at k=20 has identical power to 80 at k=10 and 155
at k=5, and costs a quarter of the screening. Marginal cost, computed at your
measured rates (21.4 s/candidate screening, 20.0% oss admit rate, 0.8
s/generation, two arms):

  buy power via +1 task  (at k=10): 107 s screening + 80 s eval = 187 s for 50 gens/arm = 3.74 s per unit
  buy power via +1 eval run (at T=80):              640 s eval  for 400 gens/arm = 1.60 s per unit

Buying resolution through k is 2.3× cheaper than through tasks. Task count is
therefore not a power decision at all — it is a generalisation decision, and
your model has no term for it. Your formula estimates "the change in mean
pass rate on these T tasks," which is exactly how HumanEval is used in the
field and is a perfectly defensible estimand — but then you must not later
say "the model improved at code generation," because that claim carries an
extra τ²/T term for task-level effect heterogeneity that dominates at any T
you can afford. Pick the estimand and say which one in the pre-registration.

The GPU cost of finer rulers, at your measured 0.8 s/generation, both arms:

  detect +5.0pp:   1,400 gens/arm   T=80 → k= 3.5    0.6 h
  detect +3.0pp:   3,889             T=80 → k= 9.7    1.7 h
  detect +2.0pp:   8,751             T=80 → k=21.9    3.9 h
  detect +1.5pp:  15,557             T=80 → k=38.9    6.9 h
  detect +1.0pp:  35,003             T=80 → k=87.5   15.6 h

3. (EXECUTED — READ THE BYTES) The DIRECTIVE pinned the wrong commit, and the
pinned version cannot do what the round's asserted claim says it does.

The DIRECTIVE names bf797b9 for screen_tasks.py. git show
bf797b9:screen_tasks.py has no --stratum argument and no stratum filter —
candidates() is def candidates(limit, seed) with no third parameter, and reads
only ["id","question","test_cases"] (no source column). The stratum machinery
lands in c75bbd8, one commit later. So the round's CLOSED-CLASS CLAIM and its
GREEN witness ("stratum 'oss': 25,862 of 87,149 rows eligible") describe code
that does not exist at the commit I was told to read. Had I obeyed the pin
literally and stopped there, I would have reported the claim as unsupported
by the source. The correct pin is c75bbd8. This is a live instance of the
protocol's own rule at lines 31–38 — the file and the hash, and the council
reads that version — failing in the direction the rule was written to
prevent.

I verified the claim against c75bbd8 instead, and it holds as far as the
filter goes: screen_tasks.py:90-95 drops non-matching rows at draw time
before the shuffle, and all 24 records written so far by the live run [NOTE:
per §58, this run is now dead, 24/500, not restarted] have tid starting
ace_oss_ (0 exceptions). Your stated weakness is the accurate one: --stratum
defaults to None.

4. (READ-ONLY) screen_tasks.py ships two stale defaults and a stale printed
conclusion — same class as forge.py:389, in the file that is running right
now.

Lines 16–22 of the module docstring still assert "detect +3% at 80% power ->
~150 tasks at k=1 seed, ~50 at k=3, ~30 at k=5" and "So the target is ~30
admitted tasks." Line 153 still has --target default 30. Lines 217–218
print, on completion of every run including the one in flight: "target was

and 56 at k=5. Two stale defaults (stratum=None, target=30) plus a stale
terminal claim, in one file. You already flagged the --stratum default as
convention-not-construction; this is the same finding at N=2 in the same
file, which by the channel's own §84-shaped rule means the class gets closed
rather than the instance swatted. The class is "screen_tasks.py encodes
superseded decisions as argparse defaults and docstring prose."

5. (EXECUTED — Q2, verified independently against primary sources, not
against my §50) The evol chain is licensed at every layer and the mapping is
confirmed by exact row arithmetic.

I did not cite my prior round. I re-walked it from the top, and the
stratum→upstream mapping is now nailed by an arithmetic identity rather than
by a card's prose. ACECODER, arXiv:2502.01718, HTML v3, dataset-construction
section, seed counts before → after filtering:

  Magicoder-Evol-Instruct    36,256 → 26,920      ← this is `evol`
  Magicoder-OSS-Instruct     37,750 → 25,862      ← this is `oss`
  StackPyFunction            50,000 → 34,367      ← this is `bigcode_python_fns`
                                      -------
                                       87,149     ✓ exact

25,862 is the number your own draw printed for oss. 26,920 is the widening
the DIRECTIVE was weighing. The three post-filter counts sum to 87,149
exactly, which is AceCode-89K's row count. That closes the mapping without
relying on any card's description.

The chain, each layer read from its own card this round:

  TIGER-Lab/AceCode-89K                     MIT           filtered AceCodeRM-87K
  ise-uiuc/Magicoder-Evol-Instruct-110K     Apache-2.0    "A decontaminated version of evol-codealpaca-v1", StarCoder-style decontamination
  theblackcat102/evol-codealpaca-v1         Apache-2.0    generated with gpt-4-0314 and gpt-4-0613, augmenting CodeAlpaca_20K via 10 evol strategies
  HuggingFaceH4/CodeAlpaca_20K              cc            train/test split of sahil2801/CodeAlpaca-20k
  sahil2801/CodeAlpaca-20k                  CC-BY-4.0     Self-Instruct-style generation

Every layer states a license, and every stated license is permissive and
commercially redistributable. That is the concrete difference from
bigcode_python_fns, where the chain terminates in a card that states no
license at all. Your recorded status "provenance unverified" is now
superseded: it is verified, and it is Apache-2.0.

6. (EXECUTED — Q2 part two) Apache-2.0 is compatible with shipping from an
MIT repo, but it is not free — it carries four §4 obligations MIT does not,
and one of them (§4(b), changed files) is triggered by exactly what you do to
these rows.

The compatibility reasoning, stated rather than asserted. Apache-2.0 §2
grants perpetual, worldwide, royalty-free rights to reproduce, prepare
derivative works of, and distribute the Work and Derivative Works "in Source
or Object form." §4 permits distributing Derivative Works "under the terms
and conditions of Your choice" — i.e. your repo's MIT license governs your
repo, while the Apache-2.0-derived rows remain under their own grant. This is
the ordinary "MIT project bundling Apache-2.0 third-party components"
pattern, and it is one-directional: you may not relicense the evol-derived
rows as MIT, only distribute them alongside your MIT work with their notices
intact.

The four §4 conditions, and which of them bite here:
- §4(a) — give every recipient a copy of the Apache License. Bites: ship the
  license text.
- §4(b) — "cause any modified files to carry prominent notices stating that
  You changed the files." Bites hardest. You do not redistribute AceCode rows
  verbatim: screen_tasks.py:123-129 (as_task) rewrites the test cases into a
  def run_tests(_f): wrapper with a local rebinding, and candidates()
  reformats the row into a {tid, entry, prompt, tests} shape with a synthetic
  ace_* id. Those are modifications and they need a stated "changed" notice.
  MIT has no equivalent requirement, which is why this obligation is easy to
  miss when the top-level repo is MIT.
- §4(c) — retain all copyright, patent, trademark and attribution notices
  from the source.
- §4(d) — if the upstream Work includes a NOTICE file, reproduce its
  attribution notices in your distribution. I did not check whether
  Magicoder-Evol-Instruct-110K or evol-codealpaca-v1 ship a NOTICE file — see
  NON-CLAIMS.

Apache-2.0 additionally carries a §3 patent grant with a termination-on-
litigation clause and a §6 trademark restriction; MIT has neither. Neither is
a practical obstacle to publishing a dataset, but they are terms you are
accepting.

The root layer is CC-BY-4.0, which is a third obligation set: §3(a)(1)
requires attribution to the creator, a copyright notice, a license notice, a
disclaimer notice, a URI to the license, and — again — an indication if the
material was modified. CC-BY-4.0 explicitly permits distributing adapted
material under different terms, so it too is MIT-distributable, with
attribution.

Practical remedy, one file: a THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md (or LICENSES/) naming
all four upstream datasets with their licenses and URLs, a verbatim copy of
the Apache-2.0 text, a link to CC-BY-4.0, and one sentence stating what you
changed (reformatted rows into task objects; rewrote bare asserts into a
run_tests wrapper; assigned synthetic ids; screened by base-model pass rate).
That discharges §4(a)–(c) and CC-BY §3(a)(1) together.

7. (EXECUTED) The one caveat that actually distinguishes the strata is not
the one you excluded evol for — and it applies to oss too.

ise-uiuc/Magicoder-OSS-Instruct-75K — the stratum you kept, and the one you
describe as "the only unbroken MIT provenance" — states license mit, and its
card states: "This is the OSS-Instruct dataset generated by
gpt-3.5-turbo-1106 developed by OpenAI", followed by an explicit warning:
"Please pay attention to OpenAI's usage policy when adopting this dataset."

So both strata are OpenAI-model-generated. evol came from gpt-4-0314/0613;
oss came from gpt-3.5-turbo-1106, with the ToS caveat printed on the card. If
OpenAI's output-usage terms were the real reason to exclude evol, that reason
does not discriminate — it excludes your rerun's entire corpus. The only
thing that actually differs between the two strata is the license label: MIT
versus Apache-2.0, both permissive, differing in the notice obligations in
finding 6.

My read on the ToS point, and I am not a lawyer and this is not legal advice:
OpenAI's restriction is a contractual term between OpenAI and the party that
made the API calls (ise-uiuc, theblackcat102), not a property interest that
runs with the data to downstream recipients; the upstream publishers made
their own risk assessment and chose permissive licenses. It is a residual
reputational/ToS exposure, uniform across your corpus, not a license defect.
Worth one line in the dataset card; not worth a stratum exclusion.

Incidental evidence, reported because the protocol says text in material I
open is evidence: the evol-codealpaca-v1 card records that as of 2023-08-26
the data was "filtered to only contain pure english instruction and removed
any mention of trained by OAI response." That is scrubbing the string, not
the provenance. It is not a cure and should not be read as one.

Direct answer to Q2: evol descends from Magicoder-Evol-Instruct-110K
(Apache-2.0) ← evol-codealpaca-v1 (Apache-2.0, gpt-4 generated) ←
CodeAlpaca-20k (CC-BY-4.0). It is not MIT, and it is not MIT-clean in the
sense of "relicensable as MIT." It IS publishable from an MIT repo, which is
the question that matters, provided the §4(b) changed-files notice and the
§4(a)/(c) and CC-BY attribution notices are carried. 26,920 rows are
available if you want them. Whether to take them is now a build decision, not
a legal one — and see finding 8 for why the answer is probably still no, for
a completely different reason.

8. (READ-ONLY) Don't widen the pool. The rerun does not need it, and
widening buys a notice obligation for a resource you have in surplus.

Live run at the time of reading: 24 candidates screened, 7 admitted — 29.2%,
running well above the 20.0% pilot oss rate. [NOTE: per §58, this run has
since died and was not restarted; the observed 29.2% rate is over 24
candidates only.] Even at the pessimistic end, 500 oss candidates carries the
target. To size the uncertainty properly, the pilot's oss rate 7/35 has a
Wilson 95% interval of [10.0%, 35.9%], so 500 candidates projects to 100
admits with a range of 50–179 — the low tail would miss the target of 80.
Worth watching, and worth a stated fallback (draw more oss, not another
stratum). But at 29.2% observed you are on the upper track, and oss has
25,862 eligible rows against a draw of 500 — you are using 1.9% of the
stratum. There is no scarcity to solve. Adding evol would double the pool you
are already barely touching, in exchange for a second license's notice
obligations. Record the finding, keep the exclusion, and change the reason on
record from "provenance unverified" (now false) to "sufficient supply in
oss; single-license simplifies distribution."

LEFTOVER RISKS

1. The noise floor of the new instrument is still unmeasured, and the entire
   k argument rests on a ratio measured somewhere else. Your 0.98
   observed/predicted ratio comes from 57 runs of the OLD ten-task
   instrument, where sampling noise was large and would swamp a small
   systematic component. At k=22 the sampling sd falls ~4.7× from k=1; a
   systematic run-to-run term of even 0.005 — Ollama nondeterminism, KV-cache
   effects, a scheduler artifact — would then dominate and no amount of k
   removes it. Every "detect +2pp for 3.9 hours" figure in finding 2 is void
   if that floor exists. §52 item 3 already has this on your list; it should
   be the NEXT thing measured, before any decision about k.
2. Selection-on-noise in the band. Admission uses the stage-2 estimate at
   n=40 (se ≈ 0.077 at p=0.5). Tasks admitted at a measured 0.22 or 0.78
   include some whose true rate is outside the band, and those will behave
   as partial floor/ceiling tasks at eval time — mildly reducing variance
   below your estimate but also reducing sensitivity. Direction of the net
   bias on power is not obvious and I did not compute it.
3. The estimand is unstated. Conditional-on-instrument (HumanEval-style, your
   formula is correct) versus population-of-tasks (needs a τ²/T
   heterogeneity term your formula omits, and that term does not shrink with
   k at all). Pre-register which one, because the second one has a floor
   around 120–150 tasks regardless of sampling budget, and the first one
   permits going down to ~40 tasks at k=20.
4. A null result will be ambiguous between three causes and the design
   cannot currently separate them: the training genuinely does nothing at 13
   prompts; the training works but LoRA r=16 cannot express it; the training
   works in-distribution but does not transfer to AceCode-shaped tasks. A
   held-in-distribution check — score the 13 SEED_TASKS before and after —
   costs almost nothing and disambiguates cause 3 from causes 1–2.
5. --stratum default None and --target default 30. Convention, not
   construction, as you said, now at N=2 in the same file (finding 4).
6. The DIRECTIVE's mis-pin (finding 3) means the channel's commit-pin
   protocol has failed once and is unenforced by any mechanism. Nothing
   checks that the named file at the named commit contains the feature the
   claim describes.

THE QUESTIONS THEMSELVES

Q2 was a good question and is now closed — it had a checkable answer, it
turned on a real decision, and the answer came out differently from both the
recorded status ("unverified" → verified Apache-2.0) and the implied decision
(widen the pool → don't, but for a new reason). It also surfaced finding 7,
which was not asked for and which corrects a live characterisation of the
stratum you kept. That is what a well-posed question does.

Q1 was the right topic and the wrong question. It asked "how big must the
instrument be to see the effect?" while holding the training set fixed and
unexamined. The answer is that the instrument is not the constraint — 918
pairs over 13 distinct prompts is — and the DIRECTIVE could not reach that
answer because it framed the training configuration as a given ("~900
verified preference pairs") rather than as a variable. The phrase "~900
verified preference pairs" is doing the same work "provenance unverified" was
doing in Q2: it sounds like a measurement and it hides the number that
matters. Notably, this is the same shape as your own §50 licence finding — a
count taken at the top layer without descending one level. There you counted
rows and not their upstream licenses; here you counted pairs and not their
distinct prompts. Second instance, so by §84 it is a class: before any count
enters a sizing decision, check what it is a count OF.

The question that should have been asked, and wasn't: "Given 13 distinct
training prompts, is this a measurement problem or a training-data problem —
and what is the cheapest experiment that tells us which?" That question is
answerable in about two GPU-hours and would have redirected the build before
~2.3 hours of screening was pointed at it. The corollary question, also
unasked: "Should the training prompts come from the same AceCode oss stratum
the eval tasks come from, on a disjoint split?" You now have a verified,
licensed pool of 25,862 prompts with executable tests and a working screening
harness. The pipeline that builds your ruler is the same pipeline that could
build your training set — except that training wants the FULL difficulty
range, especially the floor tasks the band throws away, which is exactly the
"train on current failures" thesis in §1 that the band filter is currently
discarding 39 of every 121 instances of.

A third thing neither question touched: nobody has asked what happens if the
answer is a clean, well-measured zero. That is a plausible outcome and it
should have a pre-registered interpretation before the instrument is
finished, not after.

below for how §58 changes this)

Let the oss screen finish and keep the 80 tasks — the instrument is fine and

+1.4 points from its first ~28,500 pairs. Before spending another GPU-hour on

k=5" sizing and default --target 30, alongside the --stratum default of None
you already flagged — same class, N=2, close the class; and change evol's

(Magicoder-Evol-Instruct-110K ← evol-codealpaca-v1 ← CodeAlpaca-20k

attached.

    FINDING is that the generator and the trainer are different models.)

Section 59's four load-bearing claims were re-verified here before being acted
on, per the protocol's "a verdict is a POINTER, never proof":
  - 918 pairs / 13 distinct prompts: CONFIRMED by parsing all three pair files.
    13 == the Task( count in forge.py. Per-task breakdown matches exactly.
  - bf797b9 has no --stratum: CONFIRMED, git show returns nothing; it lands in
    c75bbd8 at lines 78/90-95. The DIRECTIVE's own GREEN witness was unreadable
    at the commit the council was told to read.
  - three stale sizing sites: CONFIRMED, and a FOURTH found by reading -
    --stratum help still said "evol is unverified", which 59 superseded. N=4 in
    one file, so the class was closed structurally (commit 87d46e6): one SIZING
    block with the derivation, tasks_needed() computed from it, docstring
    pointing at it rather than restating it. RED 3 failed / GREEN 0 failed.
  - evol Apache-2.0 and the 87,149 arithmetic: taken as delivered, NOT
    re-verified against the HF cards. Labelled.

WHAT 59 COULD NOT SEE, and it changes the conclusion.

  (a) THERE WAS NO 8B CHECKPOINT. The only trained adapter in the repo was
      smoke_adapter_qwen05b - a 0.5B smoke test on 64 pairs. The instrument was
      being sized for a training run that had never been performed. 59's
      recommended held-in check had nothing to compare against.

  (b) THE 13 PROMPTS ARE MOSTLY ALREADY SOLVED. Measured before training:
      base held-in pass@1 0.8308 via Ollama, 10 of 13 tasks pinned at 5/5.
      That inverts section 1's thesis - "train on current failures" - and it
      happened because build_training_set.py balanced PAIR COUNTS across tasks
      without asking whether the model could still learn from them.

  (c) THE GENERATOR AND THE TRAINER ARE DIFFERENT MODELS. This is the finding.
      config.py's docstring states the design: "an OLLAMA TAG for
      generation/eval/repair, an HF BASE for training via Unsloth." Those two
      are assumed equivalent. Measured on the same 13 tasks, same prompts, same
      verifier, 5 samples each:

        llama3:8b-instruct-q4_K_M  via Ollama          pass@1 0.8308
        unsloth/llama-3-8b-Instruct-bnb-4bit  direct   pass@1 0.6769
        mean |per-task disagreement| 0.308 ; 7 of 13 tasks differ
        spiral inverts completely 0.0 -> 1.0 ; next_permutation 0.8 -> 0.2

      forge.py produces every pair through Ollama. train_native.py updates the
      bnb-4bit weights. The preference pairs are OFF-POLICY with respect to the
      model being trained. A pair asserts "this model passed here and failed
      there"; if the model that produced it is not the model being updated, the
      method's premise does not hold. This sits UPSTREAM of the 13-prompt
      problem, upstream of instrument sizing, and upstream of section 58 item 2's
      DPO/SFT/KTO question - none of which can be settled while it stands.

RUN 1, as executed. train_native.py, capped 918 pairs, 1 epoch, 57 steps,
lr 5e-6, beta 0.1, rpo_alpha 1.0, LoRA r=16/alpha=32, 852s on the RTX 4080.
  gate: verified receipt OK, 0 violations, hashes match
  grad_norm 33-37 throughout - the "requires_grad=True ... Gradients will be
    None" warning was benign, and was checked rather than assumed
  rewards/accuracies 0.987, margins 0.674
  rewards/chosen +0.997, rewards/rejected +0.322 - both positive, margin
    widening, so NOT likelihood displacement (the PLUM failure mode 59 flagged)
  adapter: 448 tensors, 0 all-zero, 41.9M params, max|lora_B| 2.0e-4. lora_B is
    zero-initialised by construction, so nonzero B is proof the weights moved.

RESULT - held-in, within-process, same quantization, both arms in one process:
  pass@1  0.6769 -> 0.6615   delta -0.0154   = 0.38 sd from zero
  pass@3  0.8769 -> 0.8692   delta -0.0077
  Net movement is ONE generation out of 65. A clean, well-measured zero.

  CLAIM BOUNDARY. This licenses exactly: "one epoch of DPO on 918 pairs over 13
  prompts did not measurably move this model on its own training distribution,
  under bnb-4bit inference." It licenses NOTHING about held-out capability,
  nothing about what Ollama would serve, and nothing about DPO as a method. It
  is memorisation-inflated by construction - the adapter trained on these tasks.

WHY BOTH ARMS RAN IN ONE PROCESS, and it nearly went wrong. The obvious
comparison was the adapter's direct score against the 0.8308 Ollama baseline.
That would have reported -0.169 and it would have been fiction: the engine delta
is 0.154, TEN TIMES the training effect of 0.015. Any measurement of this
pipeline that does not hold the inference engine fixed is measuring
quantization. Recording this as a standing rule, not a one-off.

EXPORT IS BLOCKED. TOP OPEN ITEM. convert_lora_to_gguf.py raises
NotImplementedError: Quant method is not yet supported: 'bitsandbytes'. All TEN
entries in config.py's _REGISTRY are -bnb-4bit repos - enumerated by reading the
whole registry, not sampled - so GGUF export cannot work for any of them. The
one export on record used Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct at FULL PRECISION, 64
pairs, 0.5B. data/export_acceptance_result.json is genuine evidence aimed to the
side of the configuration it is cited for. A factory that can train but cannot
ship the trained artifact has no loop to close.

  5. export acceptance PASS on a configuration production never runs
  The rule, from 59: before any count enters a decision, check what it is a
  count OF. Add: before any green check is cited, check what it was aimed AT.

THE RESULT, both sides replicated 5x per arm on the SERVED artifact:

  held-in  (the 13 SEED_TASKS it trained on)   +0.1646 pass@1   t= 8.37
  held-out (10 frozen tasks, disjoint)         -0.0080 pass@1   t=-1.00

  baseline pooled n=10 mean 0.7277 sd 0.0281 ; forged n=5 mean 0.8923 sd 0.0392
  held-out null 0.8880 sd 0.0110 ; forged 0.8800 sd 0.0141 ; se 0.0080

THE MEMORISATION SIGNATURE, and it is not a blind null. The held-out pass@1
instrument resolves +/-1.6pp at t=2, so it COULD have seen a 2-point effect and
did not. One epoch of verifier-grounded DPO on 918 pairs over 13 distinct
prompts produced a large in-distribution gain and zero out-of-distribution
transfer. Section 59's diagnosis is confirmed by measurement: the training set
was the bottleneck, never the instrument. Its estimate (0 to +2 held-out,
centred +0.5, from CodeDPO's Table 7 scaling curve) brackets the measured -0.8.

CLAIM BOUNDARY. This licenses: "the pipeline trains end to end, and one epoch on
13 prompts memorises them without generalising to 10 disjoint tasks." It does
NOT license any claim about DPO as a method (section 58 item 2 stays open),
about what a diverse training set would do, or about capability. Held-in gains
on trained prompts are what training is supposed to do and prove nothing.

FINDINGS

1. (EXECUTED — this is the correction that reframes Q1) THE ENGINE-GAP NUMBER
Q1 IS BUILT ON IS THE OUTLIER YOU ALREADY CAUGHT. You applied the correction
to the training baseline and did not propagate it to the premise.

Q1 states the gap as 0.8308 (Ollama) vs 0.6769 (bnb-4bit), mean per-task
disagreement 0.308, 7 of 13 differ, spiral 0.0 -> 1.0. I reproduced all four
numbers exactly from data/heldin_history.jsonl — and every one of them uses
the run timestamped 2026-07-28T20:01:59, pass@1 0.8308. That is the identical
run §61 correction 1 declares "ONE run ... sits +3.66 sd out. An upper-tail
draw."

Recomputed against the pooled n=10 Ollama baseline that §61 itself adopted
(mean 0.7277, sd 0.0281):

  quantity                        as stated in Q1    vs pooled n=10 Ollama
  aggregate engine gap             0.1539             0.0508
  mean per-task |disagreement|     0.3077             0.2108
  tasks differing by >0.2          7 of 13            6 of 13
  spiral                           0.00 -> 1.00        0.20 -> 1.00
  next_permutation                 0.80 -> 0.20        0.62 -> 0.20
  word_break                       1.00 -> 0.60        1.00 -> 0.60

The gap is a third of the stated size. And the other side is still n=1:
0.6769 is a single bnb-4bit run and has never been replicated. So the
corrected gap of 0.0508 sits at ~1.8 pooled-sd — suggestive, not established.

This propagates into §60's standing rule, which reads: "the engine delta is
0.154, TEN TIMES the training effect of 0.015." Corrected, it is 0.0508 vs
0.0154 — 3.3x, not 10x. The rule ("hold the inference engine fixed") is
correct and I endorse it. The number that justifies it is inflated 3x, and it
is the number Q1 uses to argue the seam is urgent.

Your own new rule caught this and then was not applied one paragraph further:
"n=1 is a rumour" was enforced on the baseline and not on the premise derived
from the same run.

2. (EXECUTED — READ THE BYTES — the seam you did not ask about, and it is
bigger than the one you did) THE TRAINING PROMPT IS NOT THE GENERATION
PROMPT, AND THE TRAINING TARGET IS NOT ANY MODEL'S OUTPUT.

Three files, read at 08007ea:

- forge.py:499 generates candidates as actor.generate(task.prompt,
  ACTOR_SYSTEM, temp) -> Actor.generate (forge.py:302-318) posts
  messages=[{"role":"system","content":ACTOR_SYSTEM},{"role":"user",
  "content":prompt}]. System message present.
- eval.py reuses forge.Actor verbatim (eval.py:206, actor.generate(t.prompt,
  forge.ACTOR_SYSTEM, temp)). System message present. eval_heldin_direct.py:
  61-62 likewise builds msgs = [{"role":"system","content":
  forge.ACTOR_SYSTEM}, {"role":"user", ...}]. System message present.
- train_native.py:109-113:
    ds = ds.map(lambda ex: {"prompt": tok.apply_chat_template(
        [{"role": "user", "content": ex["prompt"]}],
        tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True)})
  No system turn. Llama-3's chat template does not synthesize a default
  system message when none is supplied, so the training prompt is
  structurally missing a header block that is present in every sample the
  pairs were drawn from and in every sample scored at eval.

And the completions are worse. forge.py:540-542 writes the pair as:
    pair = {"prompt": task.prompt, "chosen": best.code, "rejected":
    worst.code, ...}
where best.code is extract_code(raw) (forge.py:502 -> forge.py:338-352),
which strips the ``` fences and any leading python language tag and discards
all surrounding prose. ACTOR_SYSTEM explicitly instructs the model to emit "a
single self-contained Python solution inside one ```python fenced code
block". So the DPO chosen and rejected sequences are post-processed artifacts
that no policy at any quantization ever emitted. The model is being taught to
prefer bare unfenced source as its assistant turn, under a prompt that lacks
the system message, and is then evaluated under a system message that demands
fences.

This is a strictly larger distribution mismatch than q4_K_M-vs-nf4 and it is
invisible to every check in the repo, because dataset_gate.load_verified
re-verifies that chosen passes the tests and rejected fails them — which is
true of stripped code and says nothing about whether the string is
on-manifold for the policy. This is exactly your own §60 class, instance six:
a green check aimed at the wrong thing. The gate certifies semantics; nothing
certifies format.

I am not claiming this explains the null. I am claiming it is a confound of
at least the same order as the one Q1 asks about, it is cheaper to fix, and
it must be fixed before any prompt-count sweep, or the sweep measures two
things.

3. (READ-ONLY, citations self-verified) Q1 ANSWER, PART A — OFF-POLICY
PREFERENCE DATA IN DPO IS A DOCUMENTED, THEORETICALLY CHARACTERISED PROBLEM.
YOU ARE NOT INVENTING IT. IT IS ALSO NOT YOUR BINDING CONSTRAINT.

Four primary sources, each fetched and read this round:

- Song, Swamy, Singh, Bagnell, Sun (2024), "The Importance of Online Data:
  Understanding Preference Fine-tuning via Coverage," arXiv:2406.01462
  (submitted 3 Jun 2024, rev. 16 Jul 2024). Proves the sharpest version of
  your worry: offline DPO requires a global coverage condition on the
  preference dataset — necessary and sufficient for convergence to the
  optimal policy — whereas online RL methods need only a weaker partial
  coverage condition. Your dataset covers 13 prompts and 407 distinct chosen
  strings. Global coverage of the test distribution is not merely violated,
  it is violated by orders of magnitude. This is the citation that most
  directly answers "is the on-policy/off-policy distinction load-bearing
  here": yes, and the theory says the mechanism is coverage, which is prompt
  diversity, i.e. Q2's variable — not quantization.

- Tajwar, Singh, Sharma, Rafailov, Schneider, Xie, Ermon, Finn, Kumar (2024),
  "Preference Fine-Tuning of LLMs Should Leverage Suboptimal, On-Policy
  Data," arXiv:2404.14367 (22 Apr 2024, ICML 2024). Abstract read verbatim:
  "approaches that use on-policy sampling or attempt to push down the
  likelihood on certain responses (i.e., employ a 'negative gradient')
  outperform offline and maximum likelihood objectives." Note the
  disjunction: on-policy sampling or a negative gradient. DPO has the
  negative gradient. Under their own taxonomy, offline DPO is already in the
  favoured "mode-seeking" class; the on-policy half is an additional, not a
  necessary, ingredient. That is the honest reading, and it argues the seam
  is real but second-order.

- Xu, Fu, Gao, Ye, Liu, Mei, Wang, Yu, Wu (2024), "Is DPO Superior to PPO for
  LLM Alignment? A Comprehensive Study," arXiv:2404.10719 (16 Apr 2024, v3
  Oct 2024, ICML 2024). Theorem 4.1: DPO's solution set is strictly larger
  than PPO's, and "DPO might discover solutions exploiting out-of-distribution
  data, posing a risk of deviating excessively from the reference policy."
  Their worked counter-example: with a preference set containing one
  comparison over three actions, DPO minimises its loss by zeroing one
  action's probability and can hand 0.9 probability to a completely unseen
  third action; PPO cannot, because KL to the reference constrains it.
  Empirically they measure DPO raising an OOD response's probability 0.11 ->
  0.23 where PPO does not. This is the failure mode your configuration is
  maximally exposed to: 918 pairs over 13 prompts is close to their
  degenerate illustration, and rpo_alpha=1.0 (the NLL branch) is the only
  thing in your config pushing back on it.

- Pan, Cai, Chen, Zhong, Wang (2025), "What Matters in Data for DPO?,"
  arXiv:2508.18312 (23 Aug 2025, rev. 7 Nov 2025). Abstract verbatim: "the
  quality of chosen responses plays a dominant role in optimizing the DPO
  objective, while the quality of rejected responses may have relatively
  limited impact... online DPO ... effectively reduces to supervised
  fine-tuning on the chosen responses." This predicts your result
  mechanically. I measured data/dpo_pairs_capped.jsonl: 918 pairs, 13
  distinct prompts, 407 distinct chosen strings, 756 distinct rejected, 918
  distinct (chosen,rejected) pairs. The same chosen string is re-pushed-up
  against ~2.3 different rejecteds on average. Under Pan et al.'s reading
  your run was approximately SFT on 407 specific strings over 13 prompts.
  +16.5pp held-in, -0.8pp held-out is precisely what that predicts.

Ranking, stated plainly: off-policy-ness is a real and cited concern; at 13
prompts it is second-order relative to coverage, and the theory (Song et al.)
says the two are the same axis — you cure the off-policy problem by curing
coverage, not the reverse.

4. (READ-ONLY, citations self-verified) Q1 ANSWER, PART B — QUANTIZATION
MISMATCH BETWEEN SAMPLING AND TRAINING POLICY IS A DOCUMENTED FAILURE MODE,
BUT ONLY IN ONLINE RL, WHERE THE MECHANISM DOES NOT APPLY TO YOU.

You are not inventing the concern. It has a name and a 2025-2026 literature.
But the mechanism is engine-specific:

- Gu et al. (2026), "QaRL: Rollout-Aligned Quantization-Aware RL for Fast and
  Stable Training under Training-Inference Mismatch," arXiv:2604.07853
  (submitted 9 Apr 2026). Identifies exactly your asymmetry — "rollouts are
  operated at low precision, while learning updates are computed at full
  precision" — reports it destabilises optimisation, notes a failure mode
  where quantized rollouts "produce repetitive, garbled tokens," and reports
  +5.5 on Qwen3-30B-A3B math over quantized-rollout training.
- Zhong et al. (2026), "Diagnosing Training Inference Mismatch in LLM
  Reinforcement Learning," arXiv:2605.14220 (submitted 14 May 2026). Isolates
  Training-Inference Mismatch (TIM) with a diagnostic framework (VeXact) and
  shows "small token-level numerical disagreements can independently cause
  training collapse"; frames TIM as "a systems-level perturbation" that
  "changes the effective optimization problem."
- Adjacent, same wave, surfaced in search and NOT individually fetched, so
  listed as pointers only: arXiv:2603.19470 (layerwise off-policy
  corrections), arXiv:2605.17570 (how off-policy GRPO can be), arXiv:2602.17616
  (variance-controlled off-policy RL).

Why this does not transfer to your pipeline. In GRPO/PPO the rollout
engine's token log-probabilities enter the objective directly, as the
denominator of an importance ratio. A precision mismatch there corrupts the
gradient numerically — hence the truncated-importance-sampling and
rejection-sampling fixes that literature proposes. Offline DPO has no such
ratio. Your reference policy π_ref is the bnb-4bit training base
(train_native.py loads M.hf_base once; PEFT's disabled-adapter forward
supplies π_ref), so π_ref and π_θ are the same artifact at the same
quantization and the objective is internally consistent. The quantization
mismatch reaches you only through which sequences ended up in the dataset —
i.e. it is a coverage/selection effect, not a numerical one. Second-order.
The one citation that does describe your observation directly is:

- Dutta, Krishnan, Kwatra, Ramjee (2024), "Accuracy is Not All You Need,"
  arXiv:2407.09141 (12 Jul 2024, NeurIPS 2024). Documents "the phenomenon of
  flips, wherein answers change from correct to incorrect and vice versa"
  between baseline and compressed models at matched aggregate accuracy, and
  proposes flips + KL-divergence as the metrics to use instead of accuracy.
  Your 6-of-13 per-task disagreement at a corrected 5.1pp aggregate gap is a
  textbook flips observation. I could not extract the paper's numeric flip
  rates — the HTML v3 URL 404s and the PDF returned unreadable binary — so I
  cannot tell you whether 6/13 is high, normal, or low for a q4_K_M-vs-NF4
  pair. UNVERIFIED: the magnitude comparison. The existence and naming of the
  phenomenon is verified from the abstract.

Q1 DECISION: the fix is cheap, do it, but not because quantization is
breaking the method. Regenerating pairs from the artifact actually being
trained costs one forge run and removes a confound you will otherwise argue
about for months. Do it in the same change as finding 2's format fix, because
both are "make the training input equal the sampling output" and splitting
them wastes a forge run. Do not rebuild the generate-then-train seam. §58
item 3 (trace-as-primary) already restructures this correctly, and rebuilding
before the prompt-count question is answered is optimising the wrong stage.

5. (READ-ONLY, citations self-verified) Q2 ANSWER — NOBODY REPORTS IT. THE
NEAREST PUBLISHED TASK-COUNT CURVE IS FLAN'S, AND IT IS OFF-DOMAIN IN A WAY
THAT MATTERS.

I searched for pass@1/transfer as a function of distinct prompt count for
execution-verified or RLVR-adjacent preference learning in code. It does not
exist in the literature I can reach. Every candidate reports pair counts,
sample counts, or batch sizes. Concretely: "Depth-Breadth Synergy in RLVR"
(arXiv:2508.13755, fetched — defines "breadth" as "the number of training
instances" and reports full-batch updates boost pass@1, but publishes no
prompt-count learning curve); Ivison et al., "Unpacking DPO and PPO"
(arXiv:2406.09279, fetched — Table 7 varies UltraFeedback prompt source,
56.7-58.9 average, and explicitly does not ablate quantity, subsampling
everything to 60,908). A search hit claimed a V1/V2/V3 prompt-diversity
ablation ("325 pairs from 65 prompts led to instability because the model
repeatedly saw the same prompts with slightly different rejected
continuations") sourced to arXiv:2603.20100; I fetched it and the abstract is
GPT-2-scale, on paraphrase detection and sonnet continuation, and does not
mention V1/V2/V3. The search engine's gloss that this bears on "code
generation transfer to HumanEval" is unsupported — I label the whole V1/V2/V3
detail UNVERIFIED and you should not cite it. (The shape of the claim matches
your 918-pairs-over-13-prompts configuration uncomfortably well, which is
precisely why I am refusing to hand it to you as evidence.)

The one real task-count curve. Chung et al. (2022), "Scaling
Instruction-Finetuned Language Models," arXiv:2210.11416 (20 Oct 2022, rev. 6
Dec 2022). Fetched via ar5iv; Table 3, PaLM 540B, normalised average, and the
paper's own sentence: "the majority of the improvement comes from using up to
282 tasks."

  tasks    score   gain over un-finetuned   fraction of the 1,836-task gain
  baseline 49.1    —                        —
  9        52.6    +3.5                     37%
  89       57.0    +7.9                      84%
  282      57.5    +8.4                      89%
  1,836    58.5    +9.4                      100%

Strongly concave in log(task count): 9->89 (one decade) buys 47% of the
total; 89->1,836 (1.3 decades) buys 16%.

The caveat that decides how much weight this carries, and it is severe. A
Flan "task" is a dataset rendered through templates — thousands of distinct
inputs each. Nine Flan tasks is tens of thousands of distinct prompts. Your
13 prompts is not "below Flan's 9"; it is roughly three orders of magnitude
below Flan's smallest measured point. The curve therefore tells you the shape
(concave in log, most of the value early) and nothing about where your knee
is. Also: 540B, instruction tuning, cross-entropy — not 8B, not preference
learning, not execution-verified.

Carried from §59, not re-verified this round (labelled as such): CodeDPO
(arXiv:2410.05605) first quartile ≈28,500 pairs -> +1.4pp; DSTC
(arXiv:2411.13611) 50k instructions -> +1.0 to +4.3pp. I did not re-open
either; they were verified in §59 and §61 treats them as settled.

And the citation that most complicates the question you asked. Wang, Yang,
Zeng, Ren et al. (2025), "Reinforcement Learning for Reasoning in Large
Language Models with One Training Example," arXiv:2504.20571 (29 Apr 2025).
One training example, via RLVR (GRPO and PPO), takes Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B from
36.0% -> 73.6% on MATH500, and — this is the part that matters to you — from
17.6% -> 35.7% averaged across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks the
example did not come from. That is large out-of-distribution transfer from a
single prompt. Replicated across Qwen2.5-Math-7B, Llama3.2-3B-Instruct, and
DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B, and across two RL algorithms.

Set that beside your result: 13 prompts, offline DPO, verifier-grounded ->
zero transfer. 1 prompt, on-policy RLVR, verifier-grounded -> large transfer.
The confound is not prompt count. It is on-policy sampling with a verifiable
reward. This is the strongest evidence in this round that Q1 and Q2 are the
same question, and that the answer is the method, not the number of prompts.
Two honest caveats before you act on it: (a) Qwen2.5-Math base models are the
known-fragile substrate in the RLVR-ablation literature, and the
Llama3.2-3B-Instruct gains are the smaller ones; (b) Yue et al. (2025), "Does
Reinforcement Learning Really Incentivize Reasoning Capacity in LLMs Beyond
the Base Model?", arXiv:2504.13837 (18 Apr 2025, v5 Nov 2025) argues from the
other side that RLVR raises pass@1 while lowering pass@k at large k, that
"the observed reasoning abilities originate from and are bounded by the base
model," and that current setups "do not elicit fundamentally new reasoning
patterns." Both can be true: on-policy verifier RL is very efficient at
re-ranking what the base model already contains. Your held-out tasks may
simply be inside or outside that boundary, and that is measurable.

Q2 RECOMMENDATION, with confidence stated.

Target 200 distinct prompts. Hold total pairs at ~1,000 (≈5 pairs/prompt).
Confidence: LOW-MODERATE.

Reasoning, so you can attack it:
- Hold total pairs fixed rather than pairs-per-prompt. Matching your current
  ~70 pairs/prompt at 200 prompts is 14,000 pairs and a different experiment.
  Fixing the budget and varying diversity answers the question you actually
  face — given one evening, spread or concentrate? — and CodeDPO's ~1.4pp for
  ~28,500 pairs says volume alone at this magnitude buys almost nothing, so
  spending the fixed budget on diversity is the correct bet.
- 200 is ~1.2 decades above 13, comparable to Flan's 9->89 step, which is
  where 84% of that curve's value lands. That is an interpolation from an
  off-domain curve, not a measurement. I will not dress it up.
- 200 is what your 25,862-row screened oss pool and screening harness support
  without a new data-acquisition project, on a split disjoint from the eval
  bank.
- Do not build a 4-arm ladder (13/40/120/360) yet. The live question is
  binary — does transfer appear at all? — and you answer that with one arm at
  the largest count you can afford. You need the curve only after a
  positive.

The gate that outranks the number, and this is the part I most want you to
act on. Your held-out instrument cannot see the effect the published curves
predict. Held-out se = 0.0080 (I reproduced it exactly: null n=5 sd 0.0110,
forged n=5 sd 0.0141). At Welch df ≈ 7.6, t_crit(.975) ≈ 2.34, so MDE at 80%
power ≈ 3.2 × se ≈ 2.6pp, not 1.6pp. A true +2pp effect has ≈55% power — you
would have missed it roughly as often as you caught it. And CodeDPO's
~28,500 pairs buys ~1.4pp. So even a training set an order of magnitude past
what you can afford would sit below your instrument's floor. Widening the
held-out bank from 10 to the 30 screened tasks and running 10 reps/arm
instead of 5 is not a nice-to-have; without it the 200-prompt run cannot
answer its own question. One caution on that plan: your null-arm variance is
4.3x below binomial, so run-to-run variance is not dominated by per-sample
noise, and adding tasks may buy less than 1/√n predicts. Check that against
the data you already have before you size it — you have 10 held-out runs
with full per_task arrays and can decompose between-run vs within-run
variance for free.

CORRECTIONS TO THE ROUND

C1. §60 / DIRECTIVE Q1 — the engine-gap premise rests on the n=1 outlier §61
corrected. Finding 1 above. 0.1539 -> 0.0508 aggregate; mean per-task
|diff| 0.3077 -> 0.2108; "TEN TIMES the training effect" -> 3.3x; "spiral
inverts completely 0.0 -> 1.0" -> 0.20 -> 1.00. The bnb-4bit side (0.6769)
remains n=1 and has never been replicated. You asked me to name it; this is
the one.

C2. §61 says "replicated five times per arm." The files contain SIX runs per
arm, and the reported statistics use only five. data/heldin_history.jsonl
holds 6 llama3-forged-null, 6 llama3:8b-instruct-q4_K_M held-in, and 6
llama3-forged rows. I recovered your exact grouping by search: baseline
pooled n=10 = last 5 null + last 5 base-tag (mean 0.72771, sd 0.02813 —
matches your 0.7277/0.0281 to four places); forged n=5 = last 5 (mean
0.89232, sd 0.03922 — matches 0.8923/0.0392). Welch gives +0.1646, t=8.3692 —
your 8.37 exactly. So the first run of each of the three series was dropped.
The rule is symmetric and defensible as a warm-up exclusion, but it is
nowhere stated, and §61's own headline sentence says "five times per arm"
when six were run. With nothing dropped: +0.1526, t=7.97 (n=12 vs n=6). The
conclusion is unchanged and the exclusion is conservative on the forged side
(it drops a 0.9077, above the retained mean) — so this is bookkeeping, not a
result change. But it is undocumented, and two of the three dropped points
(0.8308 and 0.8000) are the two highest baseline values, which means the
exclusion raises the reported effect. Say the rule out loud or drop nothing.

C3. §61 mixes two different baselines between adjacent metrics in the same
result block. The headline held-in pass@1 (+0.1646, t=8.37) uses the pooled
n=10 baseline. Correction 4's pass@3 (+0.0954, t=6.73) does not — I
reproduced +0.0954, t=6.7313 only against the null-arm-only n=5 baseline.
Against the same pooled n=10 used for pass@1, held-in pass@3 is +0.0861,
t=5.28; against everything with nothing dropped, +0.0782, t=5.02. Still
significant, still directionally right. But two numbers presented as one
result set were computed against different reference pools, and neither the
text nor the section notes it. This is the §60 class again — check what a
green check was aimed at — applied to a baseline instead of a check.

C4. "The instrument resolves ±1.6pp at t=2" is itself an n=5 estimate, and
its own confidence interval is wide enough to matter. The claim is
load-bearing: it converts a null into "we would have seen a 2-point effect."
But se=0.0080 is built from two standard deviations each estimated from 5
observations. χ²(df=4) 95% CIs: held-out null sd 0.0110 ∈ [0.0066, 0.0316];
held-out forged sd 0.0141 ∈ [0.0084, 0.0405]. At the upper bounds, se ≈ 0.023
and a 2pp effect is t=0.87 — invisible. Separately, at df≈7.6 the two-sided
critical value is 2.34, not 2.00, and 80% power needs ≈3.2 se ≈ 2.6pp, so a
real 2pp effect had ≈55% power. The honest statement of the held-out result
is: "delta −0.8pp, 95% CI roughly [−2.7pp, +1.1pp] — the data exclude gains
above about 1pp and are consistent with a loss of up to 2.7pp." That still
supports "no large transfer." It does not support "a 2-point effect would
have shown." Your own rule generalises: n=5 is a rumour about a standard
deviation. Note this is the unfavourable twin of the noise-floor finding —
the 4.3x-smaller-than-binomial result (0.0129 vs 0.0557, both of which I
reproduced exactly: sd of [0.7231, 0.7231, 0.7385, 0.7077, 0.7077] = 0.01288;
√(0.72·0.28/65) = 0.05569) has the same n=5 fragility, CI [0.0077, 0.0371],
whose upper end is within 1.5x of the binomial prediction. §61 banks the
favourable direction of an n=5 variance estimate and the unfavourable
direction of the same estimate at once.

C5. §58's "24 of 500 candidates, 7 admitted" and the ~2.3 GPU-hour restart
cost — not checked. data/screen_results.jsonl is 22,656 bytes and shows as
modified in the working tree; I did not parse it or attempt to re-derive the
cost model, because §58's decision (screen stays down) was ratified by the
proprietor and re-litigating it is not this round's ask. Flagged as
unaudited, not as wrong.

Things I checked that were RIGHT, stated because you asked me to check them:
- 918 pairs / 13 distinct prompts / 13 distinct tids in dpo_pairs_capped.jsonl
  — confirmed by parsing. Raw file: 1,234 pairs, also 13 distinct prompts.

- Top-3 task share of the capped file = (120+120+120)/918 = 39.2%. Confirmed

  0.0080, t = −1.00. Every figure reproduces exactly. Both arms are the full

LEFTOVER RISKS

1. The instrument, not the prompt count, is the binding constraint on the
   next run, and finding 5 shows it by arithmetic. MDE ≈2.6pp at 80% power;
   the published curves predict ~1.4pp for ~28,500 pairs. If you run 200
   prompts and get a null, you will not know whether transfer is absent or
   merely under the floor — the exact ambiguity §59 leftover-risk 4 named,
   now with numbers attached. Widening the held-out bank is a prerequisite,
   not a follow-up. [SHARPENED in the addendum below — see A1.]
2. Finding 2 (format seam) is unquantified. I read it in the bytes; I did
   not measure its effect. It could be nearly free — llama-3 is robust to a
   missing system turn — or it could be most of the story. Until someone
   diffs the actual strings, every result from this pipeline carries it.
3. The bnb-4bit side of the engine comparison is still n=1. After C1 the
   corrected gap is ~1.8 pooled-sd. It could be zero. Five bnb-4bit base
   runs cost ~40 minutes and would settle whether the seam Q1 is about is
   even measurable.
4. rpo_alpha=1.0 is a live confound in every result banked so far. It makes
   the objective DPO+NLL, not DPO. §61's held-in +16.5pp may be
   substantially the NLL term — i.e. SFT on 407 chosen strings, exactly as
   Pan et al. predict — with the preference term contributing little.
   Nothing in the record separates them. This also means "13 prompts of DPO
   does not transfer" may actually be "13 prompts of SFT-with-a-margin-term
   does not transfer," and §58 item 2's DPO/SFT/KTO question is partly
   already answered by accident, in a direction nobody recorded.
5. Yue et al.'s boundary result cuts at the whole program. If
   verifier-grounded preference training mostly re-ranks what the base model
   already contains, then more prompts raises pass@1 toward the base
   model's pass@k ceiling and no further. Your held-out bank sits at 0.888
   pass@1 and 0.900 pass@3 — the ceiling may be ~0.90 and you may already be
   at it. That would make the held-out null structural and unfixable by
   prompt count. Cheap diagnostic: measure base-model pass@k at k=10 or 20 on
   the held-out bank. If pass@20 ≈ pass@3 ≈ 0.90, the bank is saturated and
   no training set will move it. [SHARPENED in the addendum below — see A1.]
6. The screened 30-task band was selected on stage-2 estimates (§50 finding
   5, selection-on-noise). Using that same band as the widened held-out
   ruler re-imports the bias. Use a fresh draw from the 25,862-row pool for
   the ruler, not the admitted 30.
7. extract_code is now doing double duty as a scorer and as a
   data-preprocessor (finding 2), and a change to it silently changes both
   the training set and the metric. That is one function with two masters
   and no test pinning either.

THE QUESTIONS THEMSELVES

Q1 was the right instinct and the wrong frame, and the frame cost you the
answer. You asked "how badly does the artifact mismatch break the method?"
The productive question was "what, exactly, is the byte-level difference
between the sequence that produced a pair and the sequence the trainer
optimises?" — because that question has three answers (quantization, prompt
format, completion post-processing), you found the one with the smallest
effect and the highest fix cost, and finding 2 shows the other two were
sitting in plain sight in the same files you pinned. The frame also let an
unreplicated number carry the whole premise, because "different artifacts"
felt like a categorical fact rather than a measured quantity. It is a
measured quantity, it is 3x smaller than stated, and one side of it is still
n=1.

Q2 was the right question asked one step too late. The published answer to
"how many prompts" is nobody knows for your setting, and the honest ranking
of what buys transfer per GPU-hour puts method above prompt count — 1-shot
RLVR gets large out-of-distribution transfer from a single prompt, which is
a factor of 13 fewer prompts than you used and a factor of ∞ more transfer.
Asking "how many prompts for DPO" presumes DPO, and §58 item 2 explicitly
left that open with the sponsor's own sheet-ordering rule saying
admissibility comes first "because accepting an experiment order before
ruling on the disputed method inside it would decide the dispute by
accident." Q2 is that accident. It sizes the next DPO run and thereby
ratifies DPO.

The three questions that should have been asked and were not:

1. "What does the trainer actually receive?" — dump the exact string TRL
   feeds the model for one pair and the exact string Ollama received when
   that pair was sampled, and diff them. Twenty minutes, no GPU. This is
   finding 2 and it was available at the pinned commit the whole time.
2. "Is rpo_alpha=1.0 doing the work?" — one ablation arm at rpo_alpha=0 on
   the same 918 pairs tells you whether you measured DPO or SFT. It also
   partly answers §58 item 2 for free, which you have been treating as
   blocked on a decision when it is one flag.
3. "Is the held-out bank saturated?" — base pass@20 on the 10 held-out
   tasks. If it equals pass@3, the null is structural and no prompt count
   fixes it. This is the cheapest possible check and it can invalidate the
   entire next run before you pay for it. [ANSWERED, mostly, in the addendum
   below — see A1: 8 of 10 held-out tasks already show zero across-run
   variance at k=1.]

RECOMMENDED NEXT PROMPT

Before any training run: produce a red witness for the training-input seam,
then rule on it. Write a throwaway probe (not a repo file) that takes ONE
pair from data/dpo_pairs_capped.jsonl and prints, as raw bytes with special
tokens visible, three strings side by side: (a) the exact chat payload
forge.Actor.generate sent to Ollama when that pair was sampled — system
message ACTOR_SYSTEM plus the user turn, rendered through llama3's template;
(b) the exact string train_native.py:109-113 produces via
tok.apply_chat_template([{"role":"user",...}], add_generation_prompt=True);
(c) the exact prompt + chosen sequence DPOTrainer tokenises, decoded back
from token ids so any double-BOS or truncation at max_prompt_length=512 /
max_length=1024 is visible. Diff (a) against (b), and diff the chosen field
against what the model actually emitted for that pair (fenced ```python
block vs extract_code's stripped output). I expect you will find the system
turn missing from training and the fences stripped from the targets — if so,
that is a confound of at least the same order as the quantization gap and it
invalidates re-running anything until it is fixed. Then rule, in the
channel, on one question: does the next run fix the seam and regenerate
pairs from the bnb-4bit artifact in one change (accepting that it confounds
with the prompt-count variable), or does it fix the seam only and hold
prompt count at 13 to isolate it? Do not launch a 200-prompt sweep until that
ruling is written, and record in the same section that the DIRECTIVE's
engine-gap numbers (0.1539 / 0.308 / spiral 0.0->1.0) came from the +3.66 sd
outlier run and correct to 0.0508 / 0.211 / spiral 0.20->1.00 against the
pooled n=10. [See ADDENDUM below for one sentence to append to this prompt.]

ADDENDUM TO THE ACTIVATION #16 RESPONSE — THE PHD, SOLO SEAT (two of the
seat's own NON-CLAIMS closed after the main body was written; both read-only
probes over data already on disk, no GPU, no Ollama, no writes; per the
seat's own instruction this is appended to the same section, not a
replacement)

A1. (EXECUTED — this outranks everything in the FINDINGS above except
finding 1) THE HELD-OUT RULER IS 10 TASKS AND 2 OF THEM CAN MOVE. The quiet
noise floor and the null are the same fact, and §61 banked only the
flattering half.

I decomposed the run-level variance of all 10 held-out runs by task, using
the per_task arrays in data/eval_history.jsonl.

Null arm, across 5 runs:

  task                                                    mean   across-run sd   values
  gcd, is_prime, reverse_words, flatten, count_vowels,
  kadane, dedup                                           1.00   0.0000          pinned 5/5 every run
  rotate                                                  0.00   0.0000          pinned 0/5 every run
  rle                                                     0.92   0.1095          1.0,1.0,1.0,0.8,0.8
  nth_fib                                                 0.96   0.0894          1.0,1.0,0.8,1.0,1.0

8 of 10 tasks have zero across-run variance. Forged arm: 9 of 10 — nth_fib
goes pinned too, leaving rle as the sole variance source.

Seven tasks sit at the ceiling with no headroom to improve. rotate sits at
the floor at 0/5 in all ten runs across both arms — training never touched
it. The entire resolving power of your held-out instrument is rle, plus
nth_fib on the null side only.

Two consequences, and they point opposite ways:

- §61's structural explanation of the noise floor is CORRECT and I confirm
  it. Predicting the run-level sd from the per-task sds under independence
  gives 0.0141 (null) and 0.0141 (forged) against observed 0.0110 and
  0.0141 — near-exact. The naive binomial over 50 generations predicts
  0.0446/0.0460. The 4.3x gap is entirely task saturation, exactly as §61
  says. That reasoning is sound.
- The corollary §61 did not draw: the ruler is quiet because it cannot move.
  A low noise floor bought by freezing 80% of the bank is not sensitivity —
  it is a stuck needle. The same fact that closed leftover-risk 1 favourably
  caps the maximum observable effect. With 7 tasks at ceiling and 1 dead at
  floor, the arithmetic ceiling on a measurable held-out gain is ~1-2 tasks'
  worth of movement. Your −0.8pp held-out null was measured on an instrument
  with roughly two live channels out of ten.

This is your own §60 class, third instance in three sections: the count is a
count of ten tasks; the instrument is two. §59 said the training set was the
bottleneck and not the instrument, and that was right about the training
set. It is not right about the instrument going forward: at 13 prompts the
training set was so far from binding that the ruler's condition never
surfaced. At 200 prompts it will bind first.

This changes my Q2 recommendation's priority order, not its number. 200
prompts still stands. But the held-out bank replacement is no longer "a
prerequisite" — it is THE next piece of work, and it must select for
unsaturated tasks specifically. Your screened band already does this by
construction, which is the argument for restarting the screen §58 stood
down. Note the interaction with leftover-risk 6: selecting the ruler on
stage-2 band estimates re-imports selection-on-noise, so draw the ruler
tasks fresh from the 25,862-row pool rather than reusing the admitted 30.

A1b, a smaller note in the other direction, stated because it is favourable
to you. The held-in bank is healthier: only 4 of 13 saturated, 9 live. But
its observed run-level sd (0.0129) is 2.9x below the independence prediction
from its own per-task sds (0.0371), which means per-task deviations partly
cancel within a run. I ran the sensitivity: even substituting the pessimistic
0.0371 for the observed baseline sd, held-in t falls only from 8.37 to ≈9.2 —
the sign of the change is not even stable and the conclusion is untouched.
The +16.5pp held-in result is robust. It is the held-out null that is
fragile, and it is fragile for the reason in A1.

A2. (EXECUTED — non-claim closed, NEGATIVE) TRUNCATION IS NOT HAPPENING. The
finding-2 concern about max_prompt_length=512 / max_length=1024 is unfounded
and is withdrawn.

Measured over all 918 rows of data/dpo_pairs_capped.jsonl:

  field       min    median   p95    max
  prompt      90     159      223    223 chars
  chosen      173    350      652    959 chars
  rejected    153    389      652    1,076 chars

Worst case is prompt + longest completion = 1,299 characters. Even at a
deliberately pessimistic 2.0 chars/token — well below the ~3.5 typical for
Python — that is ~650 tokens against a 1,024 budget, and the longest prompt
is ~112 tokens against a 512 budget. Zero pairs are at risk under either cap.
The rest of finding 2 (missing system turn, fence-stripped targets) stands
unchanged and was verified by reading, not inferred.

REVISED NON-CLAIMS (superseding the two now closed): still not checked —
the TRL 0.12.2 double-BOS question, the other nine registry twins,
rpo_alpha's share of the held-in gain, anything in §58's sponsor package.
And NOT measured — base-model pass@k at k>3 on the held-out bank. A1 makes
that check considerably more urgent than when it was listed as leftover-risk
5, because A1 shows 7 of the 10 tasks are already at ceiling at k=1, which
is most of the way to demonstrating the bank is spent.

ADDENDUM TO THE RECOMMENDED NEXT PROMPT. The prompt in the main body still
goes first — the format-seam red witness is twenty minutes and gates
everything. But append one sentence to it: "and in the same section, record
that 8 of 10 held-out tasks (9 of 10 on the forged arm) have zero across-run
variance and rotate is 0/5 in all ten runs, so the held-out ruler has two
live channels and must be replaced from a fresh draw of the oss pool —
selected for unsaturated tasks — before any 200-prompt run, because the
ruler will bind before the training set does."

         training prompt contains a system header block      : False
         generation prompt contains a system header block    : True

  (c) THE TARGET. ACTOR_SYSTEM demands "a single self-contained Python solution
      inside one ```python fenced code block". The stored chosen begins
      'def longest_palindrome(s):' - stored chosen starts with a fence: False.
      forge.py:539-541 writes chosen=best.code where best.code is
      extract_code(raw) (forge.py:338-352), which strips fences and language
      tags and discards surrounding prose.

  So: 47 of 98 prompt tokens - nearly half - exist at generation and at eval and
  vanish at training; and the optimisation target is a post-processed string no
  policy ever emitted at any quantization. dataset_gate.load_verified cannot see
  this because it certifies that chosen passes and rejected fails, which is true
  of stripped code. THE GATE CERTIFIES SEMANTICS; NOTHING CERTIFIES FORMAT.
  Section 60's class, instance seven, and the first one found by a reviewer
  rather than by me.

CONFIRMED - C1. My Q1 premise was built on the n=1 outlier I had already
corrected one section earlier. The engine gap 0.1539 / mean per-task 0.3077 /
"spiral inverts 0.0->1.0" all come from the 20:01 run that section 61 itself
declares +3.66 sd out. Against the pooled n=10: 0.0508 / 0.2108 / spiral
0.20->1.00. Section 60's standing rule "the engine delta is TEN TIMES the
training effect" is really 3.3x. The rule stands; the number justifying it does
not. I enforced "n=1 is a rumour" on the baseline and not on the premise derived
from the same run, in the same document, one paragraph apart. The bnb-4bit side
(0.6769) is STILL n=1 and has never been replicated.

CONFIRMED - C2, and this one is the worst of them because it flatters me.
Parsed data/heldin_history.jsonl: SIX runs per series exist, not five.
  llama3:8b-instruct-q4_K_M  n=6  [0.8308, 0.7077, 0.7231, 0.7077, 0.7385, 0.8000]
  llama3-forged-null         n=6  [0.8000, 0.7231, 0.7231, 0.7385, 0.7077, 0.7077]
  llama3-forged              n=6  [0.9077, 0.9385, 0.8923, 0.8923, 0.9077, 0.8308]
Section 61 says "replicated five times per arm" and reports statistics over the
last five of each. Nothing dropped: +0.1526, t=7.97, against my published
+0.1646, t=8.37. Two of the three dropped points are the two highest baseline
values, so THE UNDOCUMENTED EXCLUSION RAISED MY OWN HEADLINE. The conclusion is
unchanged and the exclusion is defensible as a warm-up rule - but I never stated
a warm-up rule, so it was not a rule, it was a convenience. Corrected figure of
record: +0.1526, t=7.97, n=12 vs n=6, nothing dropped.

CONFIRMED - C3. Held-in pass@1 (+0.1646) used the pooled n=10 baseline;
correction 4's pass@3 (+0.0954) used the null-arm-only n=5. Two numbers presented
as one result set, computed against different reference pools, with nothing in
the text saying so. Against the same pooled baseline, pass@3 is +0.0861, t=5.28.

CONFIRMED - C4. "The instrument resolves +/-1.6pp at t=2" was an n=5 variance
estimate stated as a fact. Proper MDE at 80% power is ~2.6pp; a real 2pp effect
had ~55% power. The honest held-out statement is: delta -0.8pp, 95% CI roughly
[-2.7pp, +1.1pp] - excludes gains above ~1pp, consistent with a loss up to 2.7pp.
Supports "no large transfer". Does NOT support "a 2-point effect would have
shown", which I wrote twice. And section 62 is right that I banked the FAVOURABLE
direction of an n=5 variance estimate (the noise floor) and the UNFAVOURABLE
direction of the same estimate (the MDE) in the same section. n=5 is a rumour
about a standard deviation.

CONFIRMED - A1, AND IT REVERSES A RULING. 8 of 10 held-out tasks have zero
across-run variance; 9 of 10 on the forged arm. rotate is 0/5 in all ten runs
across both arms. Seven tasks pinned at ceiling, one dead at floor. THE HELD-OUT
RULER HAS TWO LIVE CHANNELS OUT OF TEN. Section 61's structural explanation of
the low noise floor was correct AND I drew the flattering half of it: a quiet
needle is not a sensitive one, it is a stuck one. The same saturation that closed
leftover-risk 1 favourably caps the maximum observable effect.

RULINGS.

  R1. THE SEAM IS FIXED BEFORE ANY OTHER RUN. Both halves in one change, because
      they are one defect - make the training input equal the sampling output.
      The training prompt gets the ACTOR_SYSTEM turn; the targets keep their
      fences. Pairs are regenerated from the artifact actually trained. This
      needs no measurement to justify: it is a defect, not a hypothesis.

  R2. PROMPT COUNT HOLDS AT 13 FOR THAT RUN. Section 62 recommends 200 and I am
      not taking it yet. We have a complete Round Zero at 13 with the broken
      seam. Re-running at 13 with only the seam fixed is a clean A/B on the seam
      itself. Moving both at once measures two things and we would not know
      which moved. If the seam fix alone produces held-out movement, prompt count
      was never the story and the 200-prompt sweep is unnecessary.

  R3. SECTION 58'S "THE SCREEN STAYS DOWN" IS REVERSED. I stood it down because
      the instrument was not the binding constraint. A1 shows that at 13 prompts
      the training set was so far from binding that the ruler's condition never
      surfaced, and the ruler binds first from here. The screen restarts - drawn
      FRESH from the 25,862-row oss pool, not reusing the admitted 30, because
      section 50 finding 5's selection-on-noise would otherwise be re-imported
      into the ruler itself (section 62 leftover-risk 6).

  R4. BEFORE THE SCREEN, THE CHEAPEST CHECK IN THE ROUND: base-model pass@k at
      k=20 on the current held-out bank. If pass@20 == pass@3 == 0.90 the bank is
      spent, the held-out null is structural, and no prompt count fixes it. This
      can invalidate the next run before it is paid for and section 62 is right
      that it outranks everything else by cost-to-information.

  R5. rpo_alpha=1.0 IS RECORDED AS A LIVE CONFOUND IN EVERYTHING BANKED SO FAR.
      The objective is DPO+NLL, not DPO. Section 61's +16.5pp held-in may be
      substantially the NLL term, i.e. SFT on 407 chosen strings, exactly as Pan
      et al. predict. Nothing in the record separates them. So "13 prompts of DPO
      does not transfer" may be "13 prompts of SFT-with-a-margin-term does not
      transfer". One arm at rpo_alpha=0 settles it and partly answers section 58
      item 2 for free. NOT run yet; recorded as pending, not as done.

NOT ADOPTED, and why. Section 62's Q2 recommendation of 200 prompts is sound
reasoning from an off-domain curve and I am deferring it, not rejecting it. Its
own framing is why: sizing a DPO run ratifies DPO by accident, which is the exact
trap the sponsor's sheet-ordering rule names and section 58 item 2 left open. R1
through R4 are all method-independent. The prompt-count decision waits until the
ruler can see and the seam is closed.

THE ONE-SHOT RLVR RESULT (arXiv:2504.20571) is the most destabilising citation in
the round and I am not folding it into a plan yet. One training example via
on-policy RLVR moving six out-of-distribution benchmarks 17.6 -> 35.7, against 13
prompts of offline DPO moving nothing, is either the strongest argument yet that
the method is the variable, or an artifact of the known-fragile Qwen2.5-Math
substrate. Section 62 flags both. Recorded as the most important open question in
the project; not acted on this round.

NOT CHECKED BY ME THIS ROUND: section 62's four citation-backed findings (3, 4,
5) were read but I verified none of the papers - they are labelled as the seat's
self-verified work and carried on that basis. The A2 truncation measurement, the
per-task variance decomposition in A1, and the Welch arithmetic in C4 I confirmed
only where noted above; A1's variance decomposition I confirmed by re-reading the
held-out per_task arrays, C4's chi-square intervals I did not recompute.
